Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.

I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.

So… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I’m just not sure how to measure that.

I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I mean, I used to look forward to little projects around the apartment, I guess, but even saying that makes me think about how I never finished organizing the closet after I bought those clear bins. They’re still stacked weirdly by the door, and I keep meaning to return two of them because the lids don’t fit right.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t know if it feels meaningful. Sometimes I start something and then I’m just… thinking about the mess around it, or whether I’m doing it wrong, and then I can’t tell if I liked it or if I was just trying to make myself feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or moment before the divorce when you truly felt pleasure or satisfaction, and have you been able to feel anything close to that lately?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one specific thing, and my mind keeps jumping around. Before the divorce… maybe there were moments, like finishing something at home and feeling like, okay, this is mine, I made this place feel decent. But then I start thinking about the apartment now and how I still haven’t fixed that one loose handle on the cabinet, and it’s such a small thing but it bothers me every time I see it.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve felt something close to that lately. Or maybe I have for a second and then it just gets swallowed up by everything else. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.\n\nEven little things, like taking a shower or opening my laptop, feel weirdly heavy. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I’m just not sure how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or interest in most activities, even those you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble saying yes or no to that. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as “most activities,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I start doing something and then I’m not really in it, but I don’t know if that means I’m not interested or if my mind is just somewhere else. Like I’ll open a book or put on a show, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid that one bill or why the city uses such inconsistent formatting on permit documents—sorry, that’s not really related.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say I no longer take pleasure in things. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.\n\nEven little things, like taking a shower or opening my laptop, feel weirdly heavy. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I’m just not sure how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or interest in most activities, even those you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble saying yes or no to that. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as “most activities,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I start doing something and then I’m not really in it, but I don’t know if that means I’m not interested or if my mind is just somewhere else. Like I’ll open a book or put on a show, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid that one bill or why the city uses such inconsistent formatting on permit documents—sorry, that’s not really related.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say I no longer take pleasure in things. I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past month when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something — even for a short moment — that you used to care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I keep coming up with maybe-not-examples.\n\nLike, I was looking at some old photos on my phone, and for a second I was interested in where one of them was taken, but then I got distracted by how many duplicates there were and started thinking I should organize the folders. Which is ridiculous, because then that becomes the activity instead of the memory.\n\nSo… genuinely engaged? I don’t know. Maybe briefly here and there, but I can’t point to a clear moment and say, yes, that was it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you have little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities — even ones you used to enjoy — most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer it that strongly. “Almost all activities” and “most of the day, nearly every day” feels very… exact, like I’d need a spreadsheet or something.\n\nI mean, I do notice I drift away from things, even things I used to like, but then I start wondering if that’s the same as not enjoying them. Like if I put on music and then I’m thinking about whether the kitchen light has been making that buzzing sound longer than I realized, was I not interested in the music or was I just distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes. I also can’t say everything feels enjoyable. I just don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I just don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of something you did recently — even something small — where you felt a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was just for a little while?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of something clear, and I keep landing on… not really sure.

Like, I made coffee the other morning and it came out right—not too bitter—and I remember thinking, okay, that’s good. But then I started wondering if I’d cleaned the filter properly, because there was that weird buildup around the edge, and then I was thinking about how the water in this building leaves deposits on everything. So was that pleasure? Or just noticing a thing was okay?

I don’t know. I can probably point to small “that’s fine” moments, but a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction… I’m having trouble identifying one.

Like I’ll put on a show or make coffee the way I like it, and then I start thinking about whether I even picked the right coffee table for the apartment, which is ridiculous because that has nothing to do with anything.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like I can’t tell what counts as enjoyment anymore. Before the divorce… maybe there were moments, like finishing something at home and feeling like, okay, this is mine, I made this place feel decent. But then I start thinking about the apartment now and how I still haven’t fixed that one loose handle on the cabinet, and it’s such a small thing but it bothers me every time I see it.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve felt something close to that lately. Or maybe I have for a second and then it just gets swallowed up by everything else. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.\n\nEven little things, like taking a shower or opening my laptop, feel weirdly heavy. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It’s hard to separate from just being tired or annoyed, but at work especially it’s been really noticeable.

Like, I’ll open a drainage calculation I’ve done a million times, or one of those spreadsheets I’ve used for years, and I just… blank. I’ll be staring at the same row of numbers, rereading it over and over, and I still don’t trust myself to decide what it means. Then I start checking the column headers, and then whether the units are right, and then I’m thinking about some old project where someone mislabeled a pipe size, which isn’t even the point.

So, yes. Even simple tasks don’t feel simple lately.

But then I start thinking about the apartment now and how I still haven’t fixed that one loose handle on the cabinet, and it’s such a small thing but it bothers me every time I see it.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve felt something close to that lately. Or maybe I have for a second and then it just gets swallowed up by everything else. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. I’m just not sure how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or interest in most activities, even those you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble saying yes or no to that. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as “most activities,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I start doing something and then I’m not really in it, but I don’t know if that means I’m not interested or if my mind is just somewhere else. Like I’ll open a book or put on a show, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid that one bill or why the city uses such inconsistent formatting on permit documents—sorry, that’s not really related.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say I no longer take pleasure in things. I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past month when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something — even for a short moment — that you used to care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I keep coming up with maybe-not-examples.\n\nLike, I was looking at some old photos on my phone, and for a second I was interested in where one of them was taken, but then I got distracted by how many duplicates there were and started thinking I should organize the folders. I don’t think I can put a number on it like that. Less than a quarter sounds… I mean, I’d have to list everything I used to enjoy, and then I’d start arguing with myself about whether something counts.

I can say it’s a lot less clear to me than it used to be, but I’m not sure I can honestly give you a percentage.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your lack of interest or pleasure in activities has been present nearly every day for most of the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say “nearly every day” cleanly. Most of the day is hard for me to judge, because the day gets chopped up into work blocks and errands and then I’m thinking about whether I actually felt interested or if I just kept moving because something had to get done.

Like yesterday, I spent twenty minutes comparing two sidewalk details that honestly don’t matter that much, and I was absorbed in it, but not in a good way exactly. More like I got caught in the gears of it. And then later I couldn’t tell you if I was uninterested in everything or just tired of deciding.

So… it’s there a lot, I think. But I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day for most of the day.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you that brought you here today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t shake this heavy feeling. It’s like this hopelessness sitting on me all the time. After the divorce I tried so hard to rebuild things, you know, like my apartment, my routines, even little stuff like picking out plates because I didn’t want the old ones, and now it feels like all of that just collapsed. Like I’m sitting under the wreckage of it and I don’t know how to get out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things the way you used to, even activities that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “enjoy” feels like a hard word to pin down right now. I mean, I still do things sometimes, or I try to. Like I’ll put on a show or make coffee the way I like it, and then I start thinking about whether I even picked the right coffee table for the apartment, which is ridiculous because that has nothing to do with anything.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like I can’t tell what counts as enjoyment anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you used to really look forward to or enjoy before all of this started, and if you’ve tried doing it lately, does it still feel meaningful or give you any sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I used to look forward to little projects around the apartment, I guess, but even saying that makes me think about how I never finished organizing the closet after I bought those clear bins. They’re still stacked weirdly by the door, and I keep meaning to return two of them because the lids don’t fit right.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t know if it feels meaningful. Sometimes I start something and then I’m just… thinking about the mess around it, or whether I’m doing it wrong, and then I can’t tell if I liked it or if I was just trying to make myself feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or moment before the divorce when you truly felt pleasure or satisfaction, and have you been able to feel anything close to that lately?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one specific thing, and my mind keeps jumping around. Before the divorce… maybe there were moments, like finishing something at home and feeling like, okay, this is mine, I made this place feel decent. But then I start thinking about the apartment now and how I still haven’t fixed that one loose handle on the cabinet, and it’s such a small thing but it bothers me every time I see it.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve felt something close to that lately. Or maybe I have for a second and then it just gets swallowed up by everything else. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.\n\nEven little things, like taking a shower or opening my laptop, feel weirdly heavy. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I’m just not sure how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or interest in most activities, even those you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble saying yes or no to that. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as “most activities,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I start doing something and then I’m not really in it, but I don’t know if that means I’m not interested or if my mind is just somewhere else. Like I’ll open a book or put on a show, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid that one bill or why the city uses such inconsistent formatting on permit documents—sorry, that’s not really related.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say I no longer take pleasure in things. I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past month when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something — even for a short moment — that you used to care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I keep coming up with maybe-not-examples.\n\nLike, I was looking at some old photos on my phone, and for a second I was interested in where one of them was taken, but then I got distracted by how many duplicates there were and started thinking I should organize the folders. Which is ridiculous, because then that becomes the activity instead of the memory.\n\nSo… genuinely engaged? I don’t know. Maybe briefly here and there, but I can’t point to a clear moment and say, yes, that was it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you have little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities — even ones you used to enjoy — most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer it that strongly. “Almost all activities” and “most of the day, nearly every day” feels very… exact, like I’d need a spreadsheet or something.\n\nI mean, I do notice I drift away from things, even things I used to like, but then I start wondering if that’s the same as not enjoying them. Like if I put on music and then I’m thinking about whether the kitchen light has been making that buzzing sound longer than I realized, was I not interested in the music or was I just distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes. I also can’t say everything feels enjoyable. I just don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you did recently — even something small — where you felt a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, and I keep landing on… not really sure.\n\nLike, I made coffee the other morning and it came out right—not too bitter—and I remember thinking, okay, that’s good. But then I started wondering if I’d cleaned the filter properly, because there was that weird buildup around the edge, and then I was thinking about how the water in this building leaves deposits on everything. So was that pleasure? Or just noticing a thing was okay?\n\nI don’t know. I can probably point to small “that’s fine” moments, but a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction… I’m having trouble identifying one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard to separate from just being tired or annoyed, but at work especially it’s been really noticeable.\n\nLike, I’ll open a drainage calculation I’ve done a million times, or one of those spreadsheets I’ve used for years, and I just… blank. I’ll be staring at the same row of numbers, rereading it over and over, and I still don’t trust myself to decide what it means. Then I start checking the column headers, and then whether the units are right, and then I’m thinking about some old project where someone mislabeled a pipe size, which isn’t even the point.\n\nSo, yes. Even simple tasks don’t feel simple lately.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re taking pleasure or interest in less than a quarter of the things you used to enjoy, or almost none at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can put a number on it like that. Less than a quarter sounds… I mean, I’d have to list everything I used to enjoy, and then I’d start arguing with myself about whether something counts.\n\nLike, I used to like walking around certain neighborhoods just looking at old retaining walls and bad curb cuts — which sounds boring, I know — but lately if I do that I’m mostly thinking about drainage or who approved something weird, not whether I’m enjoying it. And then I’m thinking, well, maybe that is interest? Or maybe it’s just habit.\n\nSo… almost none? I don’t know. That feels too absolute. I can say it’s a lot less clear to me than it used to be, but I’m not sure I can honestly give you a percentage.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of interest or pleasure in activities has been present nearly every day for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “nearly every day” cleanly. Most of the day is hard for me to judge, because the day gets chopped up into work blocks and errands and then I’m thinking about whether I actually felt interested or if I just kept moving because something had to get done.\n\nLike yesterday, I spent twenty minutes comparing two sidewalk details that honestly don’t matter that much, and I was absorbed in it, but not in a good way exactly. More like I got caught in the gears of it. And then later I couldn’t tell you if I was uninterested in everything or just tired of deciding.\n\nSo… it’s there a lot, I think. But I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day for most of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Absolutely no pleasure in anything… I don’t know that I can say that. “Anything at all” feels too big, like if I had one okay moment looking at the way a bridge joint was detailed, then that would make the whole answer false, right?

It’s more like I can’t tell what counts anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities — including things you used to enjoy — most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still getting stuck on the “clear” part. I can say things don’t land the way they used to, but if you ask me whether it’s almost all activities, most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks… I start wanting to make a chart or something, which I know is probably not helpful.

Like, I used to get real satisfaction from figuring out why a street corner feels wrong — signal timing, curb radius, the whole mess — and lately I’ll look at the same kind of thing and it’s more like I’m just… processing it. But then sometimes I’ll still get pulled into it for a minute, so does that count as interest? I don’t know.

So I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. It feels reduced, but I can’t confidently say it’s been a clear loss in almost everything for most of every day.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you that brought you here today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t shake this heavy feeling. It’s like this hopelessness sitting on me all the time. After the divorce I tried so hard to rebuild things, you know, like my apartment, my routines, even little stuff like picking out plates because I didn’t want the old ones, and now it feels like all of that just collapsed. Like I’m sitting under the wreckage of it and I don’t know how to get out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things the way you used to, even activities that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “enjoy” feels like a hard word to pin down right now. I mean, I still do things sometimes, or I try to. Like I’ll put on a show or make coffee the way I like it, and then I start thinking about whether I even picked the right coffee table for the apartment, which is ridiculous because that has nothing to do with anything.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like I can’t tell what counts as enjoyment anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you used to really look forward to or enjoy before all of this started, and if you’ve tried doing it lately, does it still feel meaningful or give you any sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I used to look forward to little projects around the apartment, I guess, but even saying that makes me think about how I never finished organizing the closet after I bought those clear bins. They’re still stacked weirdly by the door, and I keep meaning to return two of them because the lids don’t fit right.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t know if it feels meaningful. Sometimes I start something and then I’m just… thinking about the mess around it, or whether I’m doing it wrong, and then I can’t tell if I liked it or if I was just trying to make myself feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or moment before the divorce when you truly felt pleasure or satisfaction, and have you been able to feel anything close to that lately?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one specific thing, and my mind keeps jumping around. Before the divorce… maybe there were moments, like finishing something at home and feeling like, okay, this is mine, I made this place feel decent. But then I start thinking about the apartment now and how I still haven’t fixed that one loose handle on the cabinet, and it’s such a small thing but it bothers me every time I see it.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve felt something close to that lately. Or maybe I have for a second and then it just gets swallowed up by everything else. It’s hard for me to separate the actual feeling from all the noise around it.\n\nDoctor: How many days out of the week would you say you feel this heavy, hopeless feeling — and how long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: Most days, I think. It’s hard to count exactly because the days kind of blur together, but it feels like… almost every day there’s this heaviness sitting on me.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to build after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m stuck underneath it. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid things, like whether I should move the couch or fix something in the apartment, but it doesn’t really change the feeling. It’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling slowed down or like you don’t have much energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. A normal workday feels like I’m dragging wet concrete, if that makes sense. Like I can do the steps, technically, but everything takes so much effort.\n\nEven little things, like taking a shower or opening my laptop, feel weirdly heavy. I’ll sit there looking at the laptop like it’s some huge project, and then I start noticing the dust on the table or the charger cord being tangled, and suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes not doing anything. It’s not even that the task is complicated. I just don’t have the energy I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately — like eating much less than usual or losing interest in food altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether it’s less or more. Some days I realize I haven’t thought about lunch until it’s already late, but then other times I’ll just grab whatever’s easiest.\n\nFood hasn’t been something I’ve been… focused on, I guess. It’s funny, because I used to be more particular about cooking, or at least keeping certain things in the fridge, and now I’ll open it and just stand there looking at the shelves like I’m trying to solve a work problem. But I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing, exactly. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve completely lost interest in doing things you used to care about, or that nothing really feels worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels… big. I mean, there are things I used to care about, sure, but when I try to think about whether I’ve lost interest or whether I’m just tired, it gets kind of tangled.\n\nLike, I’ll look at something I meant to do and then remember I should probably deal with the mail, and then the mail reminds me of changing my address on something from months ago, and then I’m just standing there. So I don’t know if nothing feels worth doing, exactly. It’s more like I can’t always tell what I want to do in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about things you used to care about, or that nothing really brings you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I keep trying to separate “not caring” from just feeling stuck, and I don’t know if I’m doing that right.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I used to care about this thing, but then I start thinking about whether I ever cared about it properly or if it was just part of my routine. And then I remember this one project at work where everyone kept saying the design review was “high priority,” but half the files were named wrong, and I spent more time sorting folders than actually reviewing anything—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t clearly say nothing brings me pleasure. It’s more that I’m not paying attention to it the same way. I don’t know. I’d probably need you to ask it another way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost everything you do, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. “Almost everything” makes me want to start making a list, and then I get stuck on whether I’m enjoying it or just doing it because it’s there.\n\nLike, if I’m watching something, I might laugh once, but then I’m thinking about a bridge inspection report from work where the photos weren’t labeled right, and then I’m not really watching anymore. But is that not taking pleasure, or just getting distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed it in a definite way. I can’t say nothing interests me, exactly. I’m just not sure how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or interest in most activities, even those you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble saying yes or no to that. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as “most activities,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I start doing something and then I’m not really in it, but I don’t know if that means I’m not interested or if my mind is just somewhere else. Like I’ll open a book or put on a show, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid that one bill or why the city uses such inconsistent formatting on permit documents—sorry, that’s not really related.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say I no longer take pleasure in things. I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past month when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something — even for a short moment — that you used to care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I keep coming up with maybe-not-examples.\n\nLike, I was looking at some old photos on my phone, and for a second I was interested in where one of them was taken, but then I got distracted by how many duplicates there were and started thinking I should organize the folders. Which is ridiculous, because then that becomes the activity instead of the memory.\n\nSo… genuinely engaged? I don’t know. Maybe briefly here and there, but I can’t point to a clear moment and say, yes, that was it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you have little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities — even ones you used to enjoy — most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer it that strongly. “Almost all activities” and “most of the day, nearly every day” feels very… exact, like I’d need a spreadsheet or something.\n\nI mean, I do notice I drift away from things, even things I used to like, but then I start wondering if that’s the same as not enjoying them. Like if I put on music and then I’m thinking about whether the kitchen light has been making that buzzing sound longer than I realized, was I not interested in the music or was I just distracted? I don’t know.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes. I also can’t say everything feels enjoyable. I just don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you did recently — even something small — where you felt a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, and I keep landing on… not really sure.\n\nLike, I made coffee the other morning and it came out right—not too bitter—and I remember thinking, okay, that’s good. But then I started wondering if I’d cleaned the filter properly, because there was that weird buildup around the edge, and then I was thinking about how the water in this building leaves deposits on everything. So was that pleasure? Or just noticing a thing was okay?\n\nI don’t know. I can probably point to small “that’s fine” moments, but a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction… I’m having trouble identifying one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard to separate from just being tired or annoyed, but at work especially it’s been really noticeable.\n\nLike, I’ll open a drainage calculation I’ve done a million times, or one of those spreadsheets I’ve used for years, and I just… blank. I’ll be staring at the same row of numbers, rereading it over and over, and I still don’t trust myself to decide what it means. Then I start checking the column headers, and then whether the units are right, and then I’m thinking about some old project where someone mislabeled a pipe size, which isn’t even the point.\n\nSo, yes. Even simple tasks don’t feel simple lately.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re taking pleasure or interest in less than a quarter of the things you used to enjoy, or almost none at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can put a number on it like that. Less than a quarter sounds… I mean, I’d have to list everything I used to enjoy, and then I’d start arguing with myself about whether something counts.\n\nLike, I used to like walking around certain neighborhoods just looking at old retaining walls and bad curb cuts — which sounds boring, I know — but lately if I do that I’m mostly thinking about drainage or who approved something weird, not whether I’m enjoying it. And then I’m thinking, well, maybe that is interest? Or maybe it’s just habit.\n\nSo… almost none? I don’t know. That feels too absolute. I can say it’s a lot less clear to me than it used to be, but I’m not sure I can honestly give you a percentage.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of interest or pleasure in activities has been present nearly every day for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “nearly every day” cleanly. Most of the day is hard for me to judge, because the day gets chopped up into work blocks and errands and then I’m thinking about whether I actually felt interested or if I just kept moving because something had to get done.\n\nLike yesterday, I spent twenty minutes comparing two sidewalk details that honestly don’t matter that much, and I was absorbed in it, but not in a good way exactly. More like I got caught in the gears of it. And then later I couldn’t tell you if I was uninterested in everything or just tired of deciding.\n\nSo… it’s there a lot, I think. But I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day for most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take absolutely no pleasure or interest in anything at all — not even things you used to enjoy — for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure in anything… I don’t know that I can say that. “Anything at all” feels too big, like if I had one okay moment looking at the way a bridge joint was detailed, then that would make the whole answer false, right?\n\nI mean, most things feel kind of flattened out lately, but then I’ll still notice something stupid, like a well-laid bit of sidewalk or someone’s terrible grading plan, and I’ll have a reaction to it. I don’t know if that’s pleasure exactly, or just my brain being annoying and grabbing onto engineering stuff because it’s familiar.\n\nSo… no, I’m not sure I can honestly say absolutely none, most of the day nearly every day. It’s more like I can’t tell what counts anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities — including things you used to enjoy — most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still getting stuck on the “clear” part. But then sometimes I’ll still get pulled into it for a minute, so does that count as interest? I don’t know.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. So was that pleasure? Or just noticing a thing was okay?\n\nI don’t know. I can probably point to small “that’s fine” moments, but a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction… I’m having trouble identifying one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard to separate from just being tired or annoyed, but at work especially it’s been really noticeable.\n\nLike, I’ll open a drainage calculation I’ve done a million times, or one of those spreadsheets I’ve used for years, and I just… blank. I’ll be staring at the same row of numbers, rereading it over and over, and I still don’t trust myself to decide what it means. Then I start checking the column headers, and then whether the units are right, and then I’m thinking about some old project where someone mislabeled a pipe size, which isn’t even the point.\n\nSo, yes. Even simple tasks don’t feel simple lately.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re taking pleasure or interest in less than a quarter of the things you used to enjoy, or almost none at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can put a number on it like that. Less than a quarter sounds… I mean, I’d have to list everything I used to enjoy, and then I’d start arguing with myself about whether something counts.\n\nLike, I used to like walking around certain neighborhoods just looking at old retaining walls and bad curb cuts — which sounds boring, I know — but lately if I do that I’m mostly thinking about drainage or who approved something weird, not whether I’m enjoying it. And then I’m thinking, well, maybe that is interest? Or maybe it’s just habit.\n\nSo… almost none? I don’t know. That feels too absolute. I can say it’s a lot less clear to me than it used to be, but I’m not sure I can honestly give you a percentage.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your lack of interest or pleasure in activities has been present nearly every day for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “nearly every day” cleanly. Most of the day is hard for me to judge, because the day gets chopped up into work blocks and errands and then I’m thinking about whether I actually felt interested or if I just kept moving because something had to get done.\n\nLike yesterday, I spent twenty minutes comparing two sidewalk details that honestly don’t matter that much, and I was absorbed in it, but not in a good way exactly. More like I got caught in the gears of it. And then later I couldn’t tell you if I was uninterested in everything or just tired of deciding.\n\nSo… it’s there a lot, I think. But I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day for most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take absolutely no pleasure or interest in anything at all — not even things you used to enjoy — for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure in anything… I don’t know that I can say that. “Anything at all” feels too big, like if I had one okay moment looking at the way a bridge joint was detailed, then that would make the whole answer false, right?\n\nI mean, most things feel kind of flattened out lately, but then I’ll still notice something stupid, like a well-laid bit of sidewalk or someone’s terrible grading plan, and I’ll have a reaction to it. I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past month when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something — even for a short moment — that you used to care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and I keep coming up with maybe-not-examples.\n\nLike, I was looking at some old photos on my phone, and for a second I was interested in where one of them was taken, but then I got distracted by how many duplicates there were and started thinking I should organize the folders. I just don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of something you did recently — even something small — where you felt a clear sense of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, and I keep landing on… not really sure.\n\nLike, I made coffee the other morning and it came out right—not too bitter—and I remember thinking, okay, that’s good. It’s hard to separate from just being tired or annoyed, but at work especially it’s been really noticeable.\n\nLike, I’ll open a drainage calculation I’ve done a million times, or one of those spreadsheets I’ve used for years, and I just… blank. I’ll be staring at the same row of numbers, rereading it over and over, and I still don’t trust myself to decide what it means. Then I start checking the column headers, and then whether the units are right, and then I’m thinking about some old project where someone mislabeled a pipe size, which isn’t even the point.\n\nSo, yes.
